Blinded human-review packet

1 GEN-BIO-002

How are fats digested? Where do they get digested?

Required answer points

  • Bile emulsifies fats into small globules
  • Pancreatic lipase acts on fats
  • Digestion is completed in the small intestine
  • End products include fatty acids and glycerol

Optional answer points

  • The intestinal medium becomes alkaline

Prohibited / unsupported claims

  • Fats are completely digested in the stomach

Answer A

Fats are digested in the small intestine. The process begins with the liver secreting bile juice, which converts fats into small globule-like forms through a process called emulsification. This increases the surface area for further digestion. The pancreas then secretes pancreatic juice containing the enzyme lipase, which breaks down the fats into fatty acids and glycerol. These small molecules are then absorbed by the walls of the small intestine.

Food in the form of a soft slimy substance where some proteins and
carbohydrates have already been broken down is called chyme. Now the
food material passes from the stomach to the small intestine. Here the
ring like muscles called pyloric sphincters relax to open the passage into
the small intestine. The sphincters are responsible for regulating the
opening of the passage such that only small quantities of the food material
may be passed into the small intestine at a time.
The small intestine is the longest part of the
alimentary canal. Its proximal part is called duodenum.
It is the site of further digestion of carbohydrates,
proteins and fats. It receives the secretion of liver and
pancreas for this purpose. These juices render the internal
condition of the intestine gradually to a basic or alkaline one.
Fats are digested by converting them into small globule
like forms by the help of the bile juice secreted from liver.
This process is called emulsification.
Pancreatic juice secreted from pancreas contains enzymes like trypsin
for carrying on the process of digestion of proteins and lipase for fats.
Walls of the small intestine secrete intestinal juice (Succusentericus)
which carry this process further that is small molecules of proteins are
broken down to further smaller molecules. The same is the condition with
fats. Carbohydrate digestion that started in the mouth and did not occur in
the stomach, resumes now as the medium gradually changes to an alkaline
one and the enzymes become active for complete carbohydrate breakdown.

Transport of the products of digestion from the intestine into blood
(through the wall of small intestine) is called absorption. Internally,
intestinal wall has a number of finger like projections called Microvilli.
The villi increase the surface area for absorption. Blood vessels and lymph
vessels are present in the form of a network in the villi.
Products of digestion are absorbed first into the villi and from
there into the blood vessels. This means the digested food absorbed by
the walls of the small intestine. Thus after maximum absorption of food in
the small intestine the rest passes into the large intestine which is
undigested.

Answer B

retrieved

Fats are digested in the small intestine. The process begins with the secretion of bile juice from the liver, which emulsifies fats, breaking them down into smaller globules. This process is known as emulsification. The pancreatic juice, which contains the enzyme lipase, further digests these emulsified fats into fatty acids and glycerol. The small intestine also secretes intestinal juice, which continues the digestion process, ensuring fats are broken down into absorbable forms.

We eat various types of food which pass through the same digestive
tract.
It is to be converted to substances small enough to be utilised
by our body.

1.5.1 Passage of food through alimentary canal or gut
Food is cut and crushed by our teeth in the
mouth and mixed with saliva to make it wet and
slippery lump called bolus (This process is called
as mastication).
This bolus is suitable to pass
through oesophagus.
Saliva is secreted by three pairs
of salivary glands.
Saliva mainly contains an enzyme
amylase (ptyalin) which help in the breakdown of
complex carbohydrates into simple ones.
This
process of breaking down larger complex substances
into simple ones with the help of enzymes is called digestion.
The
tongue helps in mixing the food and pushing it into the next part.
The
lower jaw also helps in this process.

The soft food mixed with saliva passes through oesophagus or food
pipe by wave like movements called peristaltic movement to the stomach.
In the stomach, food gets churned with gastric juice and HCl rendering the
medium acidic.
Now the food is in semisolid condition.
The digestion of
food goes on as most of the proteins are broken down into smaller
molecules with the help of enzyme pepsin.

1.6 Health aspects of the alimentary canal
The human alimentary canal usually functions remarkably well
considering how badly we treat it on occasions!
Sometimes it rebels, and
we either feel sick or have indigestion.
V omiting is the body’s method of ridding itself of unwanted or harmful
substances from the stomach.
The peristaltic movements of the stomach
and oesophagus reverse their normal direction and the food is expelled.
There are many causes of vomiting, but one of the most common is
overeating, especially when the food contains a high proportions of fat.

Nutrition 19
V omiting also occurs when we eat something indigestible or
poisonous.
When we have a greenish vomit usually called as ‘bilious’ or ‘liverish’,
we get a bitter taste and it is often the result of overeating.
The liver is
unable to cope with the excessive fat and we get a feeling of nausea.
Indigestion is a general term used when there is difficulty in digesting
food.
Healthy people can usually avoid problems related to digestion by:
a) having simple, well balanced meals

Nutrition 23
x In human beings the food eaten is broken down in various steps with the help of enzymes secreted
by digestive glands which are associated with the alimentary canal and the digested food is absorbed
in small intestine to be sent to all cells in the body.
x The digestive system includes the alimentary tract and several associated organs and digestive
glands.
The functions of system are as follows :
a) Ingestion: Taking of food into the body
b) Digestion: Breaking up of complex food substances into the simple substances by specific
enzymes.
So that they can be used by the body.
c) Absorption: The passage of digested food through the walls of alimentary tract (particularly
in small intestine ) into circulatory system.
d) Defecation: The passage of undigested material from the body by the way of anus.

E5 X Biology EM 2025-26.pdf · pdf p.221 · book p.212 · rank 4 · chunk biology:fixed:00135
Thus the mineral matter is constantly being removed from the earth to
become a part of the plant, which may become a part of animal body.
Try to identify the basic sources of various foods we eat.
For example
curd that we eat is processed from milk, which comes from a cow, which
in turn eats grass.
The grass carries out photosynthesis and prepares food.
In every case, the origin of food materials can be traced back to green
plants.
Once the food is eaten, its energy follows a variety of pattern through
the organisms.
Not all the food can be fully digested and assimilated.
Hair,
feathers, insect exoskeletons, cartilage and bone in animal foods, cellulose
and lignin in plant foods cannot be digested by the most animals.
These
materials are either ejected by defecation or regurgitated as indigested
remains.
Assimilated energy (that is not lost through respiration or excretion)
is available for the synthesis of new biomass through growth and
reproduction.
Organisms lose some biomass by death, disease or annual
leaf-drop, where they enter the detritus pathways of the food chain i.e.,
after the death and decomposition of organisms the materials flow back
into the environment(soil, water, air).
The remaining biomass is eventually
consumed by herbivores or predators and its energy there by enters the
next higher trophic level in the ecosystem.
The materials keep on cycling i.e. entering the living beings and through
death and decay returning to the soil and atmosphere Such a flow of
materials between organisms and their environment is called Cycling of
materials or mineral circulation or Biogeochemical cycles (You have
learnt in class IX ).
Energy enters the producers in the ecosystem from the sun in
the form of solar energy or solar radiation.
No other organisms
except green plants and Photosynthetic bacteria due to due to the
presence of chlorophyll are capable of absorbing solar energy and
converting it into chemical energy (food).
Solar energy Producers (Chemical energy) Consumers (Chemical energy)
Heat Heat
Reflection of light at curved surfaces213 Our Environment
From the producers, the chemical energy passes to the consumers
from one trophic level to the next through food.
At each trophic level,
organisms use most of the food energy that they assimilate into their bodies
to fulfill their metabolic requirements- performance of work, growth and
reproduction.
Because biological energy transformations are inefficient,
a substantial proportion of metabolized food energy is lost, unused as
heat.
Hence pyramid of energy is always upright.
Only a small fraction goes to the eater at next trophic level.
Organisms
are no different from man-made machines in this respect.
Most of the
energy in petrol is lost as heat in a car’s engine rather than being
transformed into the energy of motion.
In natural communities, energy
used to perform work or dissipated as heat cannot be consumed by other
organism and is forever lost to the ecosystem.
9.4 The effects of human activities on ecosystems
In the earlier classes we have studied about different types of pollution
as a result of human interventions in ecosystem.
In this section we will try
to understand that when we cut forest to grow food crops, how this activity
brings harmful changes in ecosystem and affects organisms of each trophic
level.
Let us study a pond ecosystem to understand the components of
environment, their interactions and effects of human interventions in the
following story.

Answer C

gold

Fats are primarily digested in the small intestine. The process begins when bile juice, secreted by the liver, emulsifies fats. Emulsification breaks down large fat globules into smaller ones, making it easier for enzymes to act on them. The pancreatic juice, which contains the enzyme lipase, further digests these emulsified fats into fatty acids and glycerol. This digestion occurs in the duodenum, the first part of the small intestine, where the environment is made alkaline by the secretions from the liver and pancreas, facilitating the action of digestive enzymes.

Transport of the products of digestion from the intestine into blood
(through the wall of small intestine) is called absorption. Internally,
intestinal wall has a number of finger like projections called Microvilli.
The villi increase the surface area for absorption. Blood vessels and lymph
vessels are present in the form of a network in the villi.
Products of digestion are absorbed first into the villi and from
there into the blood vessels. This means the digested food absorbed by
the walls of the small intestine. Thus after maximum absorption of food in
the small intestine the rest passes into the large intestine which is
undigested.

Answer D

Fats are digested in the small intestine. The process begins when bile juice from the liver is released into the small intestine. This bile juice helps convert fats into small globule-like forms, a process called emulsification. This makes the fats easier for the pancreatic enzymes, such as lipase, to break them down into fatty acids and glycerol. These smaller molecules can then be absorbed by the walls of the small intestine.

1.6 Health aspects of the alimentary canal
The human alimentary canal usually functions remarkably well
considering how badly we treat it on occasions!
Sometimes it rebels, and
we either feel sick or have indigestion.
V omiting is the body’s method of ridding itself of unwanted or harmful
substances from the stomach.
The peristaltic movements of the stomach
and oesophagus reverse their normal direction and the food is expelled.
There are many causes of vomiting, but one of the most common is
overeating, especially when the food contains a high proportions of fat.

E5 X Biology EM 2025-26.pdf · pdf p.221 · book p.212 · rank 4 · chunk biology:fixed:00135
Thus the mineral matter is constantly being removed from the earth to
become a part of the plant, which may become a part of animal body.
Try to identify the basic sources of various foods we eat.
For example
curd that we eat is processed from milk, which comes from a cow, which
in turn eats grass.
The grass carries out photosynthesis and prepares food.
In every case, the origin of food materials can be traced back to green
plants.
Once the food is eaten, its energy follows a variety of pattern through
the organisms.
Not all the food can be fully digested and assimilated.
Hair,
feathers, insect exoskeletons, cartilage and bone in animal foods, cellulose
and lignin in plant foods cannot be digested by the most animals.
These
materials are either ejected by defecation or regurgitated as indigested
remains.
Assimilated energy (that is not lost through respiration or excretion)
is available for the synthesis of new biomass through growth and
reproduction.
Organisms lose some biomass by death, disease or annual
leaf-drop, where they enter the detritus pathways of the food chain i.e.,
after the death and decomposition of organisms the materials flow back
into the environment(soil, water, air).
The remaining biomass is eventually
consumed by herbivores or predators and its energy there by enters the
next higher trophic level in the ecosystem.
The materials keep on cycling i.e. entering the living beings and through
death and decay returning to the soil and atmosphere Such a flow of
materials between organisms and their environment is called Cycling of
materials or mineral circulation or Biogeochemical cycles (You have
learnt in class IX ).
Energy enters the producers in the ecosystem from the sun in
the form of solar energy or solar radiation.
No other organisms
except green plants and Photosynthetic bacteria due to due to the
presence of chlorophyll are capable of absorbing solar energy and
converting it into chemical energy (food).
Solar energy Producers (Chemical energy) Consumers (Chemical energy)
Heat Heat
Reflection of light at curved surfaces213 Our Environment
From the producers, the chemical energy passes to the consumers
from one trophic level to the next through food.
At each trophic level,
organisms use most of the food energy that they assimilate into their bodies
to fulfill their metabolic requirements- performance of work, growth and
reproduction.
Because biological energy transformations are inefficient,
a substantial proportion of metabolized food energy is lost, unused as
heat.
Hence pyramid of energy is always upright.
Only a small fraction goes to the eater at next trophic level.
Organisms
are no different from man-made machines in this respect.
Most of the
energy in petrol is lost as heat in a car’s engine rather than being
transformed into the energy of motion.
In natural communities, energy
used to perform work or dissipated as heat cannot be consumed by other
organism and is forever lost to the ecosystem.

2 GEN-BIO-005

What is double circulation? Why is it important in humans?

Required answer points

  • Blood passes through the heart twice in one complete circulation
  • Describe the pulmonary circuit
  • Describe the systemic circuit
  • Explain efficient separation/delivery of oxygenated and deoxygenated blood

Optional answer points

  • Trace the named heart chambers and major vessels

Prohibited / unsupported claims

  • Humans have single circulation

Answer A

Double circulation is a system in which blood passes through the heart twice during one complete cycle. In humans, this process is important because it ensures that blood is efficiently circulated to all parts of the body and also to the lungs for oxygenation. The double circulation consists of two parts: pulmonary circulation and systemic circulation. In pulmonary circulation, blood from the right ventricle is pumped to the lungs through the pulmonary arteries, where it picks up oxygen and releases carbon dioxide. The oxygenated blood then returns to the left atrium via the pulmonary veins. In systemic circulation, blood from the left ventricle is pumped through the aorta to the rest of the body, delivering oxygen and nutrients to tissues. The deoxygenated blood then returns to the right atrium via the veins. This double circulation ensures that the blood is continuously oxygenated and supplied with nutrients, which is essential for the proper functioning of the human body.

Circulation58Government’s Gift for Students’ Progress
William Harvey (1578-1657), an English doctor, went to Italy for
further education under Fabrici.
Harvey dissected the hearts of dead people and studied the valves
between each atrium and its ventricle. He noticed that they were one-
way valves. They allowed the blood to flow from the atrium to the
ventricle without any hinderance. However, when the heart contracts,
blood in the ventricle could not flow back into the atrium. Instead, the
blood was pushed out into the arteries.
Harvey began thinking about the valves his teacher, Fabrici, had
discovered in the leg veins. They were one-way, and they forced the blood
to move toward the heart.
He checked that by tying off and blocking different veins in animals.
He found that the veins always bulged on the side of the block away from
the heart. As though the blood was trying to flow toward the heart. This
was true of all veins.
In the arteries, the blood bulged on the heart side of any block he put
in, as though it were trying to flow away from the heart and couldn’t move
in the other direction.
Harvey now saw what was happening. The heart pushed blood in to the
arteries and the blood returned by way of the veins. It did this for both
ventricles. The blood had a double circulation. Starting from the right
ventricle blood left by way of the arteries to the lungs and returned by way
of veins to the left atrium, this is known as ‘pulmonary circulation’.
From there it flowed into the left ventricle and left by way of aorta to the
rest of the body and returned by way of the veins to the right atrium. From
here it moved into the right ventricle, this is known as ‘systemic
circulation’. As blood reaches the heart two times through pulmonary
cycle and again through systemic, it is called ‘double circulation’.
Harvey also prooved the statement wrong which states that the blood
flowing out of the heart was used up in the body and new blood was formed.
He measured how much blood the heart pumped in one contraction and
also counted the number of contractions per minute.
He found that in one hour, the heart pumped out a quantity of blood
that was three times the weight of a man. The body couldn’t use up blood
and form new blood at such a rate.

Harvey

Blood is pumped into the blood vessels at regular intervals. The tissues
do not receive the blood continuously, but in the form of spurts. When we
keep our finger at the wrist, where the artery is passing into the hand we
feel the pressure of blood moving in it. This is the ‘pulse’. The rate of the
pulse will be equal to the number of heartbeats.

3.7 Lymphatic system
Have you ever observed what happened to your feet after
overnight journey, in sitting position without moving? We feel that
our foot wear is little tight. In elders it will be clear that the lower
part of the legs will be swollen. This stage is called oedema.
x Why do our legs swell?
As blood flows through tissues and through blood capillaries
some amount of fluids and certain solid materials are constantly
flowing out of them at different junctions. Such materials are
collected and sent back into blood circulation.
We know that blood circulates in the blood vessels, pushed by
the heart. From the heart it flows into the arteries and finally into the
capillaries. To supply nutrients to the cells (tissues), the liquid
portion of the blood with nutrients flows out of the capillaries. This
is called tissue fluid.
This fluid flows through a system called as lymphatic system.
The fluid mainly returns back into the blood stream.
Lymph is the vital link between blood and tissues by which
essential substances pass from blood to cells and excretory products from
cells to blood.
Blood is a substance which contains solid and liquid particles. Lymph
is the substance that contains blood without solid particles. Tissue fluid is
lymph present in the tissues. The liquid portion after formation of blood
clot is serum.
The muscles which are attached to the skeleton (skeletal muscles) act
as pumps when they contract the pressure exerted on lympth vessel and
blood vessel and helps in pushing the lymph flowing in lymphatic vessels
and the blood flowing in veins towards the heart.
The valves that are present in the lymphatic vessels and veins stop the
reverse flow of blood. We shall read about this as the system of lymph
circulation in detail in higher classes.
Fig-12: Lymphatic
system

Answer B

Double circulation is a process in which blood passes through the heart twice during one complete cycle. In humans, this system is important because it ensures that oxygenated and deoxygenated blood are separated, allowing for efficient oxygen delivery to the body and removal of carbon dioxide. The process involves two separate circulations: pulmonary circulation and systemic circulation. Pulmonary circulation carries deoxygenated blood from the right ventricle to the lungs, where it gets oxygenated, and returns it to the left atrium. Systemic circulation then carries oxygenated blood from the left ventricle to the rest of the body, where it delivers oxygen and picks up carbon dioxide, before returning to the right atrium. This separation of blood ensures that the body receives a continuous supply of oxygen and nutrients while efficiently removing waste products.

Right atrium and Right V entricle is referred to as “Tricuspid Valve”.
The
valve present on the left Auriculo-ventricular septum between left atrium
and left ventricle is referred as “Bicuspid valve” or “Mitral valve”.
A
major blood vessel that originates from right ventricle is Pulmonary aorta.
The valves present at the region of Pulmonary aorta are called “Pulmonary
valves”.
A major blood vessel that originates from left ventricle is
systemic aorta.
The valves present at the region of systemic aorta are
called “Systemic valves”.
In the right atrium we can observe the openings of superior and inferior
venacava.
They bring deoxygenated blood from body parts.
In the left
atrium, we can observe the openings of pulmonary veins, that bring
oxygenated blood from lungs.
Aorta arises from the upper part of the left ventricle.
It supplies
oxygenated blood to the body parts.
From the upper part of the right ventricle
pulmonary artery arises that supplies de-oxygenated blood to the lungs.
After careful examination we can observe valves in the pulmonary artery
and aorta as well.
3.3 Blood vessels and circulation
Let us study about the structure and functions of the blood vessels
along with their discovery.
It was not until 16th century that we really came to know how our blood
vessels function.
In 1574, an Italian doctor, Girolamo Fabrici, was studying
the veins in the leg.
He noticed that they had small valves in them that
permited blood to flow towards heart.
These were one-way valves that
checked backflow of blood.
Movement of leg muscle helps in movement
of blood through veins.
Everyone thought that the blood leaving the left ventricle always moved
away from the heart for which Fabrici paid no attention.
He missed the
importance of his own discovery.
Circulation58
William Harvey (1578-1657), an English doctor, went to Italy for
further education under Fabrici.
Harvey dissected the hearts of dead people and studied the valves
between each atrium and its ventricle.
He noticed that they were oneway valves.
They allowed the blood to flow from the atrium to the
ventricle without any hinderance.
However, when the heart contracts,
blood in the ventricle could not flow back into the atrium.
Instead, the
blood was pushed out into the arteries.
Harvey began thinking about the valves his teacher, Fabrici, had
discovered in the leg veins.
They were one-way, and they forced the blood
to move toward the heart.
He checked that by tying off and blocking different veins in animals.
He found that the veins always bulged on the side of the block away from
the heart.
As though the blood was trying to flow toward the heart.
This
was true of all veins.
In the arteries, the blood bulged on the heart side of any block he put
in, as though it were trying to flow away from the heart and couldn’t move
in the other direction.
Harvey now saw what was happening.
The heart pushed blood in to the
arteries and the blood returned by way of the veins.
It did this for both
ventricles.
The blood had a double circulation.
Starting from the right
ventricle blood left by way of the arteries to the lungs and returned by way
of veins to the left atrium, this is known as ‘pulmonary circulation’.
From there it flowed into the left ventricle and left by way of aorta to the
rest of the body and returned by way of the veins to the right atrium.
From
here it moved into the right ventricle, this is known as ‘systemic
circulation’.
As blood reaches the heart two times through pulmonary
cycle and again through systemic, it is called ‘double circulation’.
E2 X Biology EM 2025-26.pdf · pdf p.67 · book p.58 · rank 1 · chunk biology:fixed:00035
The blood had a double circulation.
Starting from the right
ventricle blood left by way of the arteries to the lungs and returned by way
of veins to the left atrium, this is known as ‘pulmonary circulation’.
From there it flowed into the left ventricle and left by way of aorta to the
rest of the body and returned by way of the veins to the right atrium.
From
here it moved into the right ventricle, this is known as ‘systemic
circulation’.
As blood reaches the heart two times through pulmonary
cycle and again through systemic, it is called ‘double circulation’.
Harvey also prooved the statement wrong which states that the blood
flowing out of the heart was used up in the body and new blood was formed.
He measured how much blood the heart pumped in one contraction and
also counted the number of contractions per minute.
He found that in one hour, the heart pumped out a quantity of blood
that was three times the weight of a man.
The body couldn’t use up blood
and form new blood at such a rate.

Harvey
Circulation59
The same blood had to circulate and be used over and over again.
Harvey still had some problem.
The arteries and veins that could
be seen had to be connected by vessels too small to see.
Were they
really there?
In the 1650s, scientists had learned to put lenses together in
such a way that objects too small to see with the naked eye could be
magnified and made visible.
Marcello Malpighi (1628-1694), used
the microscope to see tiny blood vessels that were invisible with
naked eye.
Later, Malpighi studied the wings of bats.
He could see
blood vessels in their thin membranes under the microscope;
he could see that the smallest arteries and veins were connected
by very fine blood vessels.
He called these blood vessels “capillaries” from the Latin
word for “hair”, because they were as thin as the finest of hairs.
With the discovery of capillaries, the idea of the circulation

ever since.

3.7 Lymphatic system
Have you ever observed what happened to your feet after
overnight journey, in sitting position without moving?
We feel that
our foot wear is little tight.
In elders it will be clear that the lower
part of the legs will be swollen.
This stage is called oedema.
x Why do our legs swell?
As blood flows through tissues and through blood capillaries
some amount of fluids and certain solid materials are constantly
flowing out of them at different junctions.
Such materials are
collected and sent back into blood circulation.
We know that blood circulates in the blood vessels, pushed by
the heart.
From the heart it flows into the arteries and finally into the
capillaries.
To supply nutrients to the cells (tissues), the liquid
portion of the blood with nutrients flows out of the capillaries.
This
is called tissue fluid.
This fluid flows through a system called as lymphatic system.
The fluid mainly returns back into the blood stream.
Lymph is the vital link between blood and tissues by which
essential substances pass from blood to cells and excretory products from
cells to blood.
Blood is a substance which contains solid and liquid particles.
Lymph
is the substance that contains blood without solid particles.
Tissue fluid is
lymph present in the tissues.
The liquid portion after formation of blood
clot is serum.
The muscles which are attached to the skeleton (skeletal muscles) act
as pumps when they contract the pressure exerted on lympth vessel and
blood vessel and helps in pushing the lymph flowing in lymphatic vessels
and the blood flowing in veins towards the heart.
The valves that are present in the lymphatic vessels and veins stop the
reverse flow of blood.
We shall read about this as the system of lymph
circulation in detail in higher classes.
Fig-12: Lymphatic
system

3.8 Evolution of the transport (circulatory) system
When the unicellular organisms separated themselves from the sea
with the formation of the limiting membrane, the problem of
transportation arose.
The nature has found the solution, by creating a
microscopic ocean which has its own currents.
In unicellular organisms like Amoeba the protoplasm shows natural
movements.
These movements are called Brownian movements,
because of which the nutrients and oxygen are distributed throughout
the protoplasm equally.
This simplest intracellular transportation system, present in
unicellular animals has been retained in multicellular animals including
humans.
The protoplasm of any cell in our body is mobile and
protoplasmic currents exist even in the nerve cells.
The multicellular
animals have to develop more complicated system for transportation

The rhesus factor
There is another antigen of red blood cells which is present in 85% of the people of
Britain, this is known as the Rhesus factor, as it was first discovered in Rhesus monkeys.
People who have this are said to be Rhesus positive (Rh+).
Those who do not have this
factor are termed Rhesus negative (Rh-).
Normally they do not carry an antibody to this
factor in their plasma.
However, if Rh+ blood is transfused into the blood of a Rh- person,
against Rh-factors will be formed and these are capable of destroying Rh+ red cells.
Under
certain circumstances this is a potential hazard for babies.
If a Rh+ man marries a Rh- woman, some of the children are likely to be Rh+.
At birth
there is always some mixing of blood between the circulation of mother and baby and this
may occasionally happen during pregnancy.
So, if a child is Rh+ some of its blood will leak
into its mother’s circulation and cause antibodies to form in her blood.
If the mother has
more children, not all will necessarily be Rh+, but if they are, the amount of antibodies in
her blood often increases with each pregnancy, and in some instances the antibodies in her
blood may pass into the baby’s blood in sufficient quantities to produce very serious anaemia
and even death.
Fortunately these cases are not frequent, and when they do occur, the baby
is given a complete transfusion soon after birth so that the baby’s blood is replaced by
blood containing no antibodies to the rhesus factor.
It is now possible for this transfusion
to be carrried out before birth.
Another recently developed technique is for the mother to
be given an injection shortly after the birth of her first child which prevents the Rh+ cells
from stimulating the production of the harmful antibody.
Choose the correct answer
Annexure-I

Answer C

retrieved

Double circulation refers to the process where blood passes through the heart twice during one complete cycle of circulation. In humans, this involves two main pathways: pulmonary circulation and systemic circulation. 1. **Pulmonary Circulation**: Blood is pumped from the right ventricle of the heart to the lungs via the pulmonary arteries. In the lungs, the blood releases carbon dioxide and picks up oxygen. The oxygenated blood then returns to the left atrium of the heart through the pulmonary veins. 2. **Systemic Circulation**: The oxygen-rich blood moves from the left atrium to the left ventricle, from where it is pumped out to the rest of the body through the aorta. As the blood circulates through the body, it delivers oxygen and nutrients to tissues and collects carbon dioxide and other waste products. The deoxygenated blood returns to the right atrium of the heart via the superior and inferior vena cava, completing the cycle. Double circulation is important in humans because it ensures efficient oxygenation of blood and separation of oxygenated and deoxygenated blood, which is crucial for maintaining high metabolic rates and supporting complex bodily functions. This system allows for a more efficient supply of oxygen to body tissues and organs, which is essential for sustaining the energy demands of the human body. [E1] PDF 66; textbook 57 [E2] PDF 67; textbook 58

The rhesus factor
There is another antigen of red blood cells which is present in 85% of the people of
Britain, this is known as the Rhesus factor, as it was first discovered in Rhesus monkeys.
People who have this are said to be Rhesus positive (Rh+).
Those who do not have this
factor are termed Rhesus negative (Rh-).
Normally they do not carry an antibody to this
factor in their plasma.
However, if Rh+ blood is transfused into the blood of a Rh- person,
against Rh-factors will be formed and these are capable of destroying Rh+ red cells.
Under
certain circumstances this is a potential hazard for babies.
If a Rh+ man marries a Rh- woman, some of the children are likely to be Rh+.
At birth
there is always some mixing of blood between the circulation of mother and baby and this
may occasionally happen during pregnancy.
So, if a child is Rh+ some of its blood will leak
into its mother’s circulation and cause antibodies to form in her blood.
If the mother has
more children, not all will necessarily be Rh+, but if they are, the amount of antibodies in
her blood often increases with each pregnancy, and in some instances the antibodies in her
blood may pass into the baby’s blood in sufficient quantities to produce very serious anaemia
and even death.
Fortunately these cases are not frequent, and when they do occur, the baby
is given a complete transfusion soon after birth so that the baby’s blood is replaced by
blood containing no antibodies to the rhesus factor.
It is now possible for this transfusion
to be carrried out before birth.
Another recently developed technique is for the mother to
be given an injection shortly after the birth of her first child which prevents the Rh+ cells
from stimulating the production of the harmful antibody.
Choose the correct answer
Annexure-I

Answer D

gold

Double circulation refers to the process where blood flows through the heart twice during one complete cycle of circulation. In humans, this involves two main pathways: pulmonary circulation and systemic circulation. In pulmonary circulation, blood is pumped from the right ventricle of the heart to the lungs via the pulmonary arteries. In the lungs, the blood picks up oxygen and releases carbon dioxide. The oxygen-rich blood then returns to the left atrium of the heart through the pulmonary veins. In systemic circulation, the oxygenated blood is pumped from the left ventricle into the aorta and distributed to the rest of the body. After delivering oxygen and nutrients to the tissues and collecting waste products, the deoxygenated blood returns to the right atrium of the heart through the veins. Double circulation is important in humans because it ensures efficient oxygenation of blood and separation of oxygen-rich and oxygen-poor blood, which is crucial for maintaining high metabolic rates and supporting complex bodily functions.

Circulation58Government’s Gift for Students’ Progress
William Harvey (1578-1657), an English doctor, went to Italy for
further education under Fabrici.
Harvey dissected the hearts of dead people and studied the valves
between each atrium and its ventricle. He noticed that they were one-
way valves. They allowed the blood to flow from the atrium to the
ventricle without any hinderance. However, when the heart contracts,
blood in the ventricle could not flow back into the atrium. Instead, the
blood was pushed out into the arteries.
Harvey began thinking about the valves his teacher, Fabrici, had
discovered in the leg veins. They were one-way, and they forced the blood
to move toward the heart.
He checked that by tying off and blocking different veins in animals.
He found that the veins always bulged on the side of the block away from
the heart. As though the blood was trying to flow toward the heart. This
was true of all veins.
In the arteries, the blood bulged on the heart side of any block he put
in, as though it were trying to flow away from the heart and couldn’t move
in the other direction.
Harvey now saw what was happening. The heart pushed blood in to the
arteries and the blood returned by way of the veins. It did this for both
ventricles. The blood had a double circulation. Starting from the right
ventricle blood left by way of the arteries to the lungs and returned by way
of veins to the left atrium, this is known as ‘pulmonary circulation’.
From there it flowed into the left ventricle and left by way of aorta to the
rest of the body and returned by way of the veins to the right atrium. From
here it moved into the right ventricle, this is known as ‘systemic
circulation’. As blood reaches the heart two times through pulmonary
cycle and again through systemic, it is called ‘double circulation’.
Harvey also prooved the statement wrong which states that the blood
flowing out of the heart was used up in the body and new blood was formed.
He measured how much blood the heart pumped in one contraction and
also counted the number of contractions per minute.
He found that in one hour, the heart pumped out a quantity of blood
that was three times the weight of a man. The body couldn’t use up blood
and form new blood at such a rate.

Harvey

3.7 Lymphatic system
Have you ever observed what happened to your feet after
overnight journey, in sitting position without moving? We feel that
our foot wear is little tight. In elders it will be clear that the lower
part of the legs will be swollen. This stage is called oedema.
x Why do our legs swell?
As blood flows through tissues and through blood capillaries
some amount of fluids and certain solid materials are constantly
flowing out of them at different junctions. Such materials are
collected and sent back into blood circulation.
We know that blood circulates in the blood vessels, pushed by
the heart. From the heart it flows into the arteries and finally into the
capillaries. To supply nutrients to the cells (tissues), the liquid
portion of the blood with nutrients flows out of the capillaries. This
is called tissue fluid.
This fluid flows through a system called as lymphatic system.
The fluid mainly returns back into the blood stream.
Lymph is the vital link between blood and tissues by which
essential substances pass from blood to cells and excretory products from
cells to blood.
Blood is a substance which contains solid and liquid particles. Lymph
is the substance that contains blood without solid particles. Tissue fluid is
lymph present in the tissues. The liquid portion after formation of blood
clot is serum.
The muscles which are attached to the skeleton (skeletal muscles) act
as pumps when they contract the pressure exerted on lympth vessel and
blood vessel and helps in pushing the lymph flowing in lymphatic vessels
and the blood flowing in veins towards the heart.
The valves that are present in the lymphatic vessels and veins stop the
reverse flow of blood. We shall read about this as the system of lymph
circulation in detail in higher classes.

system
3 GEN-BIO-012

Write the differences between mitosis and meiosis?

Required answer points

  • Mitosis has one division; meiosis has two
  • Mitosis forms two daughter cells; meiosis forms four
  • Mitosis preserves chromosome number; meiosis halves it
  • Mitosis supports growth/repair; meiosis forms gametes
  • Meiosis creates variation through recombination/segregation

Optional answer points

  • Compare prophase events and homologous chromosomes

Prohibited / unsupported claims

  • Meiosis produces genetically identical diploid cells

Answer A

Mitosis and meiosis are two types of cell division, but they have several key differences. Mitosis occurs in all body cells and results in two daughter cells that are genetically identical to the parent cell. It retains the same number of chromosomes (2n). Meiosis, on the other hand, occurs only in sex cells (gametes) and results in four daughter cells that have half the number of chromosomes (n). This is called reduction division. Mitosis is a single process, while meiosis consists of two phases. Additionally, meiosis involves the pairing and exchange of genetic material between homologous chromosomes, which does not occur in mitosis. These differences are important for understanding how organisms grow, repair, and reproduce.

142 Reproduction
Cells in some organs, such as heart and brain of an individual may
divide slowly.
On the other hand bone marrow cells may actively divide to
produce red blood cells, which have a short life span nearly 120 days in
the body.
For example, if you cut your finger and bleed, soon a blood clot
forms to stop the bleeding.
This brings in various chemicals to the site
that stimulate skin cells to divide and heal the wound.
Cell division ceases
as the wound is completely healed.
In contrast, cancer cells do not respond
to such growth regulating factors and continuously divide at the expense
of normal cells, thus ultimately killing the host.
So it becomes important
to understand the processes involved in cell division.
The cell cycle will
help us understand this better.
6.14 Cell cycle
The process of cell division is a
very small part of the cell cycle.
It
completes in 40-60 minutes.
The
period between two cell divisions is
called ‘Interphase’.
This is actually the
period when the genetic material
(DNA) makes its copy so that it is
equally distributed to the daughter cells
during mitosis.
Interphase can be
divided into three phases.
1) G1 phase: This is the linking
period between the completion of
mitosis and the beginning of DNA replication (Gap 1 phase).
The
cell size increases during this period.
2) S phase: This is the period of DNA synthesis (Synthesis phase)
leading duplication of chromosomes.
3) G2 phase: This is the time between the end of DNA replication and
the beginning of mitosis.(Gap 2 phase).
Cell organelles divide and
prepare chromosome for mitosis.

6.14.1 Meiosis
Unlike mitosis which is a continuous process for division in most of
the cells.

Meiosis has two phases.
During the first phase of meiosis
the parent cell containing two sets of chromosomes (diploid) divides and
daughter cells containg one set of chromosomes (haploid) are formed.
The second phase of meiosis is similar to normal mitosis.
Thus the four
daughter cells have just half the number of chromosomes of the parent cell.

Nuclear membrane disappears.

(Note: No pairing of chromosomes as in meiosis).

Chromosomes move to cell equator, spindle fibres attached to
centromeres.
3.
Anaphase 1.
Centromeres split, separating the chromatids.
2.
Spindle fibres attached to centromeres contract, pulling chromatids
towards poles
4.

Chromatids elongate, become invisible, replication at this stage to
become chromosomes.
2.
Nuclear membranes form round daughter nuclei.
3.
Necleolus is resynthesized in each daughter nucleus.
Cytokinesis 1.
Nucleus divides into two and division of cytoplasm starts Two cells
are formed.
2.
Cell membrane pinches to form daughter cells (animals) or new cell
wall material becomes laid down across spindle equator (plants)

176 Heredity and Evolution
When we observe our world and its myriad forms of life, we are struck
by two seemingly opposite observations, the fantastic variety of life and
the similarity between them.
As we shall see, we would need to understand
these two characteristics of life in order to comprehend how life evolves.
When we say that something evolves, we mean not only that it changes,
but that there is also some component of direction in that change.
But, how does evolution take place?
Does it occur in a slow and steady
manner or in quick jumps?
Is it just about change and producing something
new and different?
In the chapter on reproduction we have studied that reproductive
processes usually give rise to individuals that have some new characters
in spite of the similarity that they share with their parents.
Often such new
characters give rise to observable changes in life forms.
x How are new characters produced?
x Are they inherited?
x Do they have any role in the process of evolution?
In this chapter we shall discuss all of the above.

and your mother?

characters like colour of eye(cornea), colour of hair, type of hair, shape of
nose, shape of face, type of ear lobe(attached or free), inner thumb markings
etc.

Answer B

Mitosis and meiosis are both processes of cell division, but they have several key differences. Mitosis is the process by which a single cell divides into two identical daughter cells. It occurs in somatic (body) cells and is responsible for growth, repair, and asexual reproduction. Meiosis, on the other hand, is a specialized form of cell division that occurs in the reproductive organs to produce gametes (sperm and eggs) for sexual reproduction. Meiosis results in four daughter cells, each with half the number of chromosomes as the parent cell. Mitosis involves one division, while meiosis involves two successive divisions. Additionally, meiosis includes a process called crossing over, where genetic material is exchanged between homologous chromosomes, increasing genetic diversity. In contrast, mitosis does not involve crossing over. Mitosis ensures genetic continuity, while meiosis ensures genetic variation.

Cells in some organs, such as heart and brain of an individual may
divide slowly. On the other hand bone marrow cells may actively divide to
produce red blood cells, which have a short life span nearly 120 days in
the body. For example, if you cut your finger and bleed, soon a blood clot
forms to stop the bleeding. This brings in various chemicals to the site
that stimulate skin cells to divide and heal the wound. Cell division ceases
as the wound is completely healed. In contrast, cancer cells do not respond
to such growth regulating factors and continuously divide at the expense
of normal cells, thus ultimately killing the host. So it becomes important
to understand the processes involved in cell division. The cell cycle will
help us understand this better.
6.14 Cell cycle
The process of cell division is a
very small part of the cell cycle. It
completes in 40-60 minutes. The
period between two cell divisions is
called ‘Interphase’. This is actually the
period when the genetic material
(DNA) makes its copy so that it is
equally distributed to the daughter cells
during mitosis. Interphase can be
divided into three phases.
1) G1 phase: This is the linking
period between the completion of
mitosis and the beginning of DNA replication (Gap 1 phase). The
cell size increases during this period.
2) S phase: This is the period of DNA synthesis (Synthesis phase)
leading duplication of chromosomes.
3) G2 phase: This is the time between the end of DNA replication and
the beginning of mitosis.(Gap 2 phase). Cell organelles divide and
prepare chromosome for mitosis.

M phase: This is cell division phase. It includes prophase, metaphase,
anaphase, telophase and cytokinesis.
To understand the functional relationship between these phases, Potu
Narasimha Rao and Johnson (see annexure) conducted some experiments
using the cell fusion technique. That is combining two cells in experimental
conditions. With this cell fusion technique they revealed for the first time
the structure of interphase (G1, S and G2) chromosomes that are not
ordinarily visible under the microscope. They provided evidence on
progression of cells through the cell cycle in sequential unidirectional
and controlled way by a series of chemical signals that can diffuse freely
between nucleus and cytoplasm. These experiments are considered to be
a ‘mile stone’ in the cell cycle studies.

6.14.1 Meiosis
Unlike mitosis which is a continuous process for division in most of
the cells. Meiosis occurs only during the formation of gametes in sexual
reproduction. Meiosis has two phases. During the first phase of meiosis
the parent cell containing two sets of chromosomes (diploid) divides and
daughter cells containg one set of chromosomes (haploid) are formed.
The second phase of meiosis is similar to normal mitosis. Thus the four
daughter cells have just half the number of chromosomes of the parent cell.
Stage Description
1. Prophase 1. Chromosomes condense and get coiled. They become visible even in
light microscope and nucleoli become smaller.
2. Chromosomes split lengthwise to form chromatids, connected by
centromeres.
3. Nuclear membrane disappears.
4. Centrosome, containing rod-like centrioles, divide and form ends
of spindle (probably animal cells only).
(Note: No pairing of chromosomes as in meiosis).
2. Metaphase 1. Chromosomes move to cell equator, spindle fibres attached to
centromeres.
3. Anaphase 1. Centromeres split, separating the chromatids.
2. Spindle fibres attached to centromeres contract, pulling chromatids
towards poles
4. Telophase 1. Chromatids elongate, become invisible, replication at this stage to
become chromosomes.
2. Nuclear membranes form round daughter nuclei.
3. Necleolus is resynthesized in each daughter nucleus.
Cytokinesis 1. Nucleus divides into two and division of cytoplasm starts Two cells
are formed.
2. Cell membrane pinches to form daughter cells (animals) or new cell
wall material becomes laid down across spindle equator (plants)

Answer C

retrieved

Mitosis and meiosis are two types of cell division processes that serve different purposes in living organisms. 1. **Purpose**: - **Mitosis**: This process occurs in all body cells and is responsible for growth, repair, and asexual reproduction. It ensures that each daughter cell receives an identical set of chromosomes as the parent cell. - **Meiosis**: This process occurs only in sex cells (gametes) and is crucial for sexual reproduction. It reduces the chromosome number by half, resulting in haploid cells, which is essential for maintaining the species' chromosome number through generations. 2. **Chromosome Number**: - **Mitosis**: The chromosome number remains the same as the parent cell. If the parent cell is diploid (2n), the daughter cells will also be diploid. - **Meiosis**: The chromosome number is halved. A diploid parent cell (2n) produces haploid (n) daughter cells. 3. **Number of Divisions**: - **Mitosis**: Involves one division cycle, resulting in two daughter cells. - **Meiosis**: Involves two division cycles (meiosis I and meiosis II), resulting in four daughter cells. 4. **Genetic Variation**: - **Mitosis**: Produces genetically identical cells, with no variation from the parent cell. - **Meiosis**: Increases genetic variation through processes like crossing over and independent assortment, which shuffle genetic material. 5. **Occurrence**: - **Mitosis**: Occurs in somatic (body) cells. - **Meiosis**: Occurs in germ cells during the formation of gametes (sperm and eggs). These differences highlight the distinct roles that mitosis and meiosis play in the life cycle of organisms, with mitosis focusing on growth and maintenance, and meiosis ensuring genetic diversity and proper chromosome number in offspring.

176 Heredity and Evolution
When we observe our world and its myriad forms of life, we are struck
by two seemingly opposite observations, the fantastic variety of life and
the similarity between them.
As we shall see, we would need to understand
these two characteristics of life in order to comprehend how life evolves.
When we say that something evolves, we mean not only that it changes,
but that there is also some component of direction in that change.
But, how does evolution take place?
Does it occur in a slow and steady
manner or in quick jumps?
Is it just about change and producing something
new and different?
In the chapter on reproduction we have studied that reproductive
processes usually give rise to individuals that have some new characters
in spite of the similarity that they share with their parents.
Often such new
characters give rise to observable changes in life forms.
x How are new characters produced?
x Are they inherited?
x Do they have any role in the process of evolution?
In this chapter we shall discuss all of the above.

8.2 Mendel and his experiments
Over centuries variations and their role in nature have been studied by
naturalists.
During early 19th century, a lot of work was done by several
scientists.
Some of these studies will help us to understand how variations
occur and are transferred from one generation to the next.
We shall study
a detailed account of experimental evidences provided by Gregor Johann
Mendel in the early 19th century who is known as ‘Father of genetics’.
Reflection of light at curved surfaces179 Heredity and Evolution
In 1857 Gregor Johann Mendel started working on the problem of
how variations were passed from one generation to the other.
Mendel
did not do his experimental work either in a University or in a
Laboratory.
As he was an Austrian monk in a monastery he simply did
Fig-2: Gregor
Johann Mendel
his experiments in the monastery garden.
He worked for over seven
years after which he presented the conclusions from his experimental
data in the form of a detailed research paper.
8.2.1 Reasons for selecting Pea plant
Mendel made many careful observations of plants and found that
pea plants would be most suitable to carry on further experimentation
because it has the following advantages:
1.
Well defined characters 2.
Bisexual flowers
3.
Predominently self pollinating
4.
Suitable for cross pollination
5.
It is an annual plant
Then he planned and designed experiments to find out the answers to
questions that came to his mind.
He had worked on nearly 10,000 pea
plants of 34 different varieties.
Observing pea plants carefully, Mendel noted that they differ from
one another in many ways.

Answer D

gold

Mitosis and meiosis are two different types of cell division processes. Here are the differences between them: 1. **Purpose**: - **Mitosis**: It is involved in the growth and repair of tissues. It helps in producing two identical daughter cells from a single parent cell, maintaining the same number of chromosomes as the parent cell. - **Meiosis**: It occurs during the formation of gametes in sexual reproduction. It reduces the chromosome number by half, resulting in four haploid daughter cells. 2. **Number of Divisions**: - **Mitosis**: Involves one division cycle, resulting in two daughter cells. - **Meiosis**: Involves two division cycles (Meiosis I and Meiosis II), resulting in four daughter cells. 3. **Chromosome Number**: - **Mitosis**: The daughter cells have the same diploid number of chromosomes as the parent cell. - **Meiosis**: The daughter cells have half the haploid number of chromosomes compared to the parent cell. 4. **Genetic Variation**: - **Mitosis**: Produces genetically identical cells, with no variation. - **Meiosis**: Increases genetic variation through processes like crossing over and independent assortment. 5. **Occurrence**: - **Mitosis**: Occurs in somatic (body) cells. - **Meiosis**: Occurs in germ cells during the formation of gametes. These differences are crucial for understanding how organisms grow, repair tissues, and reproduce.

142 ReproductionGovernment’s Gift for Students’ Progress
Cells in some organs, such as heart and brain of an individual may
divide slowly. On the other hand bone marrow cells may actively divide to
produce red blood cells, which have a short life span nearly 120 days in
the body. For example, if you cut your finger and bleed, soon a blood clot
forms to stop the bleeding. This brings in various chemicals to the site
that stimulate skin cells to divide and heal the wound. Cell division ceases
as the wound is completely healed. In contrast, cancer cells do not respond
to such growth regulating factors and continuously divide at the expense
of normal cells, thus ultimately killing the host. So it becomes important
to understand the processes involved in cell division. The cell cycle will
help us understand this better.
6.14 Cell cycle
The process of cell division is a
very small part of the cell cycle. It
completes in 40-60 minutes. The
period between two cell divisions is
called ‘Interphase’. This is actually the
period when the genetic material
(DNA) makes its copy so that it is
equally distributed to the daughter cells
during mitosis. Interphase can be
divided into three phases.
1) G1 phase: This is the linking
period between the completion of
mitosis and the beginning of DNA replication (Gap 1 phase). The
cell size increases during this period.
2) S phase: This is the period of DNA synthesis (Synthesis phase)
leading duplication of chromosomes.
3) G2 phase: This is the time between the end of DNA replication and
the beginning of mitosis.(Gap 2 phase). Cell organelles divide and
prepare chromosome for mitosis.
G1 (10.5 hrs)
S (10.5 hrs)
G1
S
G2
M
G2 (3.5 hrs)
M (1 hr)
Fig-28: Cell cycle
E2 X Biology EM 2025-26.pdf · pdf p.152 · book p.143
Reflection of light at curved surfacesReproduction143Government’s Gift for Students’ Progress
M phase: This is cell division phase. It includes prophase, metaphase,
anaphase, telophase and cytokinesis.
To understand the functional relationship between these phases, Potu
Narasimha Rao and Johnson (see annexure) conducted some experiments
using the cell fusion technique. That is combining two cells in experimental
conditions. With this cell fusion technique they revealed for the first time
the structure of interphase (G1, S and G2) chromosomes that are not
ordinarily visible under the microscope. They provided evidence on
progression of cells through the cell cycle in sequential unidirectional
and controlled way by a series of chemical signals that can diffuse freely
between nucleus and cytoplasm. These experiments are considered to be
a ‘mile stone’ in the cell cycle studies.

Table-1: Mitosis
6.14.1 Meiosis
Unlike mitosis which is a continuous process for division in most of
the cells. Meiosis occurs only during the formation of gametes in sexual
reproduction. Meiosis has two phases. During the first phase of meiosis
the parent cell containing two sets of chromosomes (diploid) divides and
daughter cells containg one set of chromosomes (haploid) are formed.
The second phase of meiosis is similar to normal mitosis. Thus the four
daughter cells have just half the number of chromosomes of the parent cell.
Stage Description
1. Prophase 1. Chromosomes condense and get coiled. They become visible even in
light microscope and nucleoli become smaller.
2. Chromosomes split lengthwise to form chromatids, connected by
centromeres.
3. Nuclear membrane disappears.
4. Centrosome, containing rod-like centrioles, divide and form ends
of spindle (probably animal cells only).
(Note: No pairing of chromosomes as in meiosis).
2. Metaphase 1. Chromosomes move to cell equator, spindle fibres attached to
centromeres.
3. Anaphase 1. Centromeres split, separating the chromatids.
2. Spindle fibres attached to centromeres contract, pulling chromatids
towards poles
4. Telophase 1. Chromatids elongate, become invisible, replication at this stage to
become chromosomes.
2. Nuclear membranes form round daughter nuclei.
3. Necleolus is resynthesized in each daughter nucleus.
Cytokinesis 1. Nucleus divides into two and division of cytoplasm starts Two cells
are formed.
2. Cell membrane pinches to form daughter cells (animals) or new cell
wall material becomes laid down across spindle equator (plants)
E4 X Biology EM 2025-26.pdf · pdf p.154 · book p.145
Reflection of light at curved surfacesReproduction145Government’s Gift for Students’ Progress
Hence meiosis is also called as Reduction Division. The daughter
cells are haploid in condition. You will learn more about this in further
classes.

takes place while general body growth is still going on. Therefore,
some degree of sexual maturation does not necessarily mean that the body
or the mind is ready for sexual acts. Further, it is not fit for having and
bringing up children. How do we decide if the body or the mind is ready
for this major responsibility? All of us are under many different kinds of
pressures about these issues. There can be pressure from our friends for
participating in many activities, whether we really want or not. There
can be pressure from families to get married and start having children.

4 GEN-BIO-014

Describe the acid-and-leaf experiment used to model how the stomach is protected from its own acid, and compare the observations with the stomach’s mucus lining.

Required answer points

  • Use two similar green leaves
  • Coat one with petroleum jelly/grease and leave one uncoated
  • Add equal drops of weak acid and observe
  • The uncoated leaf is affected while the coated leaf is protected
  • Petroleum jelly models the protective mucus lining of the stomach

Optional answer points

  • Observe after about half an hour

Prohibited / unsupported claims

  • Mucus neutralises all stomach acid completely

Answer A

The acid-and-leaf experiment is designed to demonstrate how the stomach is protected from its own acid. In this experiment, two similar green leaves are taken. One leaf is coated with petroleum jelly or grease, while the other is left untreated. A weak acid is then applied to both leaves. After some time, it is observed that the leaf without petroleum jelly is affected by the acid, while the leaf coated with petroleum jelly remains unaffected. This is because the petroleum jelly acts like the mucus lining in the stomach, which protects the stomach walls from the corrosive effects of stomach acid. The mucus secreted by cells in the stomach walls forms a thin lining that counteracts the acid, similar to how petroleum jelly protects the leaf from the acid.

166 Coordination in life processesGovernment’s Gift for Students’ Progress
Digestive juices are produced depending on the quantity of food
material. If the stomach would produce same amount of digestive juices
irrespective of the food quantity the walls of stomach would be destroyed.
( See annexure )
We also know that stomach secretes strong acids during digestion.
The HCl secreted by the walls of the stomach is strong enough to digest
the hard bones as well. Then how is the stomach protected from the
secretions of its own acids. To understand this we will perform the
following experiment.
Acid and leaf experiment
Take two similar green leaves from your school garden. Apply vaseline
or petroleum jelly or grease on one leaf leave the other free. Add 1 or 2
drops of some weak acid on both the leaves. Observe them after half an
hour or so and write your observation in your note book.
x What kind of change did you observe in the leaves?
x Which leaf was affected by the acid?
x What saved the other leaf from the effect of acid?
Mucus secreted by some cells present in the walls of the stomach
form a thin lining on the walls of the stomach. This counters the action of
acid. The function of petroleum jelly can be compared to that of mucus
lining the stomach walls. Hence the stomach is protected from damage
being caused by the secretion of its own acids.
7.1.8 Travel of food from the stomach to the intestine
Now food is a soup like mixture when it leaves the stomach and enters
the small intestine. When the food enters the intestine the acidic nature of
the chyme initiates the production of hormones like secretin and
cholecystokinin which stimulate pancreas, liver and walls of small intestine
to secrete pancreatic juice, bile juice and succus entericus.
x Why small intestine is like a long coiled structure?
x How the absorbtion takes place in the small intestine?
The absorption of nutrients by villi in the small intestine is a very
selective process.

Answer B

gold

The acid-and-leaf experiment is designed to model how the stomach is protected from its own acid. In this experiment, two similar green leaves are used. One leaf is coated with vaseline or petroleum jelly, while the other is left untreated. A few drops of a weak acid are added to both leaves. After some time, observations are made to see the effects of the acid on the leaves. The leaf without the protective coating is affected by the acid, showing signs of damage. In contrast, the leaf with the vaseline remains unaffected. This demonstrates how the vaseline acts as a protective barrier against the acid. This experiment is analogous to how the stomach protects itself. The stomach secretes strong acids for digestion, but it is protected by a mucus lining. This mucus acts like the vaseline, forming a barrier that prevents the acid from damaging the stomach walls. Thus, the mucus lining is crucial in protecting the stomach from its own acidic secretions.

Answer C

The acid-and-leaf experiment is used to model how the stomach is protected from its own acid. In this experiment, two similar green leaves are taken. One leaf is coated with petroleum jelly or grease, while the other is left untreated. A weak acid is then applied to both leaves. After some time, the leaf without the grease or petroleum jelly is affected by the acid, while the leaf coated with grease remains unaffected. This is because the grease acts like the mucus lining in the stomach, which protects the stomach walls from the strong acid. The mucus lining in the stomach forms a thin protective layer that counteracts the action of the acid, similar to how petroleum jelly protects the leaf. This experiment demonstrates that the mucus lining in the stomach is crucial for protecting it from its own digestive juices.

It is the bolus moving from a part near the stomach of

It is reverse peristalsis.
Though it is a common process
in ruminants such as the cow, buffalo etc. that have an extra pouch in the
stomach to store quickly swallowed food, in human beings it is mainly a
protective mechanism to expell unwanted substances from the food canal.

individuals and time after different meals.)

which is elastic in nature.

food that we intake.

organs because it is exposed to a large variety of
physiochemical stimuli from the external world in
the form of ingested food.

168 Coordination in life processes
The neural apparatus of our digestive tract comprises of such a vast
and complicated network of neurons that it has been nicknamed by scientists
as the second brain!
Research in this area is currently investigating how
the second brain mediates the body’s immune response; after all, at least
70 percent of our immune system is aimed at the gut to expel and kill
foreign invaders.
Scientists are also working to find out how trillions of bacteria in the
gut communicate with the cells of gut nervous system.
A deeper
understanding of this mass of neural tissue, filled with important
'neurotransmitters', is revealing that it does much more than merely handle
digestion or inflict the occasional nervous pang of hunger.
The little brain
in our inner yards, in connection with the big one in our skull, partly
determines our mental state and plays key roles in certain diseases

food canal.

Answer D

retrieved

The acid-and-leaf experiment is designed to model how the stomach is protected from its own acid. In this experiment, two similar green leaves are used. One leaf is coated with vaseline or petroleum jelly, while the other is left untreated. A few drops of weak acid are added to both leaves. After some time, observations are made to see which leaf is affected by the acid. The leaf without the protective coating is damaged by the acid, while the leaf with the vaseline remains unaffected. This demonstrates how a protective barrier can prevent damage from acid. In the human stomach, a similar protective mechanism is in place. The stomach walls secrete mucus, which forms a thin lining that protects the stomach from its own strong acids, such as hydrochloric acid (HCl). This mucus lining acts like the vaseline in the experiment, preventing the acid from damaging the stomach tissue.

Have you observed a ruminating cow/ buffalo under a tree or somewhere
else?
Carefully observe its neck and throat.
Do you see something moving
from its throat to mouth?
After that, the cow or buffalo starts chewing.
This is
called as rumination.
It is the bolus moving from a part near the stomach of
the animal to its mouth.
It is reverse peristalsis.
Though it is a common process
in ruminants such as the cow, buffalo etc. that have an extra pouch in the
stomach to store quickly swallowed food, in human beings it is mainly a
protective mechanism to expell unwanted substances from the food canal.
We observe that digestion of food starts from the mouth.
While the
food passes through the gut the food settles for some time for digestion
at certain locations.
So food does not move uniformly through the digestive
system.
Let us observe the time period.
(These are only averages.
The movement of materials varies among
individuals and time after different meals.)
Our stomach is not like a bag with specific volume.
It is like a pouch
which is elastic in nature.
The size of the stomach increases based on the
food that we intake.

Digestive juices are produced depending on the quantity of food
material.
If the stomach would produce same amount of digestive juices
irrespective of the food quantity the walls of stomach would be destroyed.
( See annexure )
We also know that stomach secretes strong acids during digestion.
The HCl secreted by the walls of the stomach is strong enough to digest
the hard bones as well.
Then how is the stomach protected from the
secretions of its own acids.
To understand this we will perform the
following experiment.
Acid and leaf experiment
Take two similar green leaves from your school garden.
Apply vaseline
or petroleum jelly or grease on one leaf leave the other free.
Add 1 or 2
drops of some weak acid on both the leaves.
Observe them after half an
hour or so and write your observation in your note book.
x What kind of change did you observe in the leaves?
x Which leaf was affected by the acid?
x What saved the other leaf from the effect of acid?
Mucus secreted by some cells present in the walls of the stomach
form a thin lining on the walls of the stomach.
This counters the action of
acid.
The function of petroleum jelly can be compared to that of mucus
lining the stomach walls.
Hence the stomach is protected from damage
being caused by the secretion of its own acids.
7.1.8 Travel of food from the stomach to the intestine
Now food is a soup like mixture when it leaves the stomach and enters
the small intestine.
When the food enters the intestine the acidic nature of
the chyme initiates the production of hormones like secretin and
cholecystokinin which stimulate pancreas, liver and walls of small intestine
to secrete pancreatic juice, bile juice and succus entericus.

The digestive tract is unique among internal
organs because it is exposed to a large variety of
physiochemical stimuli from the external world in
the form of ingested food.
As a consequence, the
intestine has developed a rich store of co ordinated
movements of its muscular apparatus along with
neural apparatus to ensure the appropriate mixing and

168 Coordination in life processes
The neural apparatus of our digestive tract comprises of such a vast
and complicated network of neurons that it has been nicknamed by scientists
as the second brain!
Research in this area is currently investigating how
the second brain mediates the body’s immune response; after all, at least
70 percent of our immune system is aimed at the gut to expel and kill
foreign invaders.
Scientists are also working to find out how trillions of bacteria in the
gut communicate with the cells of gut nervous system.
A deeper
understanding of this mass of neural tissue, filled with important
'neurotransmitters', is revealing that it does much more than merely handle
digestion or inflict the occasional nervous pang of hunger.
The little brain
in our inner yards, in connection with the big one in our skull, partly
determines our mental state and plays key roles in certain diseases

Reflection of light at curved surfaces171 Coordination in life processes
x A special nervous system that exists in the gut consist of nearly 100 million nerves that coordinates
the muscular activity ,blood flow,digestion and absorption of nutrients and other activities of the
food canal (gastro intestinal tract).
x The hormone Ghrelin secreted in the stomach is responsible for hunger generating sensations.
Another hormone leptin that gets secreted suppresses hunger.
x Taste can be identified easily only when the tongue is pressed against the palate.
x Taste and smell are closely related.
The chemoreceptors present in the nose and the tongue trigger
signals in the form of nerve impulses to the brain where the smell and taste is detected.
x The saliva secreted maintains an alkaline medium that aids in digestion of starch.
Our mouth secretes
acid as well ,this gives protection to our mouth from harmful bacteria etc., Under the action of
autonomous nervous system Saliva released by the salivary glands moistens the food to make
chewing and swallowing easier.
x The muscular and sensory organ in the oral cavity is the tongue which is not only gustatory in
function but also performs different functions including, shifting and mixing the food in the oral
cavity and swallowing.
x The mechanism for swallowing is coordinated by the swallowing centre in the brain stem.
x Contraction and relaxation of the muscles in the gut brings in a wave like motion that propels the
food forward and is called peristalsis.
This is a muscular wave that travels the entire length of the
food canal.
This is involuntary and under the control of autonomous nervous system as well as gut
nervous system.
x The muscular contractions of the stomach churns the food into a semiliquid substance known as
chyme.
Entry of chyme into the duodenum is regulated by a muscle called as the pyloric sphincter.
x The strong acid (HCl) renders the pH in stomach acidic causing the protein digesting enzymes to
swing into function.
x Juices secreted in the stomach breaks down the food into a smooth mixture called chyme.
x The mucus lining of the stomach protects it from damage by its own acids.
x The coordination among the processes of digestion, respiration and circulation is necessary for
utilization and oxidation of food and transport of the nutrients.
Muscular and nervous control helps
to carry out the processes in a regulated manner.

5 GEN-PSC-002

Find the distance of the image, when an object is placed on the principal axis, at a distance of 10 cm in front of a concave mirror whose radius of curvature is 8 cm.

Required answer points

  • Use f = R/2 = -4 cm and u = -10 cm under the textbook sign convention
  • Apply 1/f = 1/v + 1/u
  • Obtain v = -20/3 cm ≈ -6.67 cm
  • Interpret the negative sign as a real image on the object side

Optional answer points

  • The image is inverted and diminished between F and C

Prohibited / unsupported claims

  • Report v as +6.7 cm while also calling it a real same-side image

Answer A

retrieved

To find the distance of the image when an object is placed on the principal axis of a concave mirror, we can use the mirror formula: \[ \frac{1}{f} = \frac{1}{v} + \frac{1}{u} \] where: - \( f \) is the focal length of the mirror, - \( v \) is the image distance, - \( u \) is the object distance. Given: - The object distance \( u = -10 \) cm (the negative sign is due to the sign convention for mirrors), - The radius of curvature \( R = 8 \) cm. The focal length \( f \) is half of the radius of curvature for a concave mirror: \[ f = \frac{R}{2} = \frac{8}{2} = 4 \text{ cm} \] Substituting the values into the mirror formula: \[ \frac{1}{4} = \frac{1}{v} + \frac{1}{-10} \] Solving for \( \frac{1}{v} \): \[ \frac{1}{v} = \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{10} \] \[ \frac{1}{v} = \frac{5}{20} + \frac{2}{20} = \frac{7}{20} \] \[ v = \frac{20}{7} \approx 2.86 \text{ cm} \] Thus, the image distance \( v \) is approximately 2.86 cm in front of the mirror, indicating a real and inverted image.

E1 X Physics EM 2025-26.pdf · pdf p.13 · book p.3 · rank 4 · chunk physical_sciences:fixed:00001
A concave mirror will be like the rubber sole bent inwards (fig-1(b)) and
the convex mirror will be like the rubber sole bent out wards (fig-1(c)).
For a concave mirror, like these pins in fig.-1( b), all normals will
converge towards a point.
This point is called centre of curvature(C) of
the mirror.
This gives us a clue about how we can find normal at a point on a
spherical mirror.
All that we have to do is to draw a
line from the point on the mirror to centre of the
sphere.
It is much easier to imagine this in a two
dimensional fig. as shown in fig.-2(a).
The concave
mirror is actually a part of a big sphere.
In order to
find this centre point (centre of curvature) we have to
think of the centre of the sphere to which the concave
mirror belongs.
The line drawn from C to any point on
the mirror gives the normal at that point.
For the ray R, the incident angle is the angle it
makes with the normal shown as i and the reflected
angle is shown as r in fig.-2(b).
We know by first law of reflection i = r.
The mid point (Geometrical centre) of the mirror is called pole (P)
of the mirror.
The horizontal line shown in the fig.s which passes through

1.1.1 Verifying your drawing
with experiment:
To verify this we must first find out
some way of obtaining a beam of parallel
rays.
How do we do that?
First we need to know the situation
in which one gets parallel rays of light.
In the fig.-3 we have stuck two pins on a thermocole block.
The pins
are parallel to each other.
As we see in the fig., when a source of light is
kept very near, we see the shadows diverging (from the base of the pins).
As we move the source away from the pins, the angle of divergence gets
reduced.
If we move the source far away we will get parallel shadows.
But
as we move the candle away, the light intensity becomes low.
That means
to get a beam of parallel rays the source should be at a long distance and it
must be of sufficient intensity.
Where do we find one such source?
Yes, we have one easily available source, you probably have guessed
it: The Sun.
Let us do an experiment with sun rays and a concave mirror.
